The ARVN fights 1974 and the defence of Phuoc Long with plans written for an air force that no longer comes, and loses Thuong Duc and Phuoc Long as history did, with its officers knowing exactly what they lack.

#### The Paris Agreement
*27 Jan 1973 · Paris · historical · EV000-83*
On 27 January 1973 the Agreement on Ending the War and Restoring Peace in Vietnam was signed in Paris by the United States, the Democratic Republic of Vietnam, the Republic of Vietnam and the Provisional Revolutionary Government. A ceasefire in place took effect on 28 January Saigon time. The United States and its allies would withdraw all forces within 60 days, and prisoners would be exchanged in the same period. Nothing required the PAVN to leave the South, where by US estimates about 150,000 of its troops remained. The Thieu government remained, and a National Council of National Reconciliation and Concord was to arrange elections, which it never did. An International Commission of Control and Supervision of Canadian, Hungarian, Indonesian and Polish personnel, and a Four-Party Joint Military Commission, were to supervise the ceasefire, and both sides could replace worn-out armaments one for one. Thieu signed under American pressure, with Nixon's written promises of continued aid and of a forceful response to a major violation³³⁵. The agreement was close to the terms of October 1972.

#### The ceasefire that never held
*28 Jan – Dec 1973 · The countryside of South Vietnam · historical · EV000-84*
In the last days before the ceasefire both sides grabbed for hamlets and roads, raising flags to claim territory, the "war of the flags"; the ARVN recovered most of what the PAVN and VC had seized. Fighting resumed in earnest by March³³⁵. Through most of 1973 the Saigon government was on the offensive: the Communists, rebuilding supply lines and wary of provoking the return of American air power, largely stayed on the defensive, while the ARVN and territorial forces pushed into PRG-held areas. Both sides violated the agreement from the start; the ICCS could not stop it, and Canada left the commission in July 1973. From late 1973 the PAVN grew bolder as the American political system turned on itself, and by early 1974 the two sides fought roughly on equal terms³⁵⁷.

#### Operation Homecoming and the last American troops
*12 Feb – 29 Mar 1973 · Hanoi, Clark Air Base and Saigon · historical · EV000-85*
On 12 February 1973 the first American prisoners were flown out of Hanoi to Clark Air Base in the Philippines. By 29 March, 566 US servicemen, 23 American civilians and 5 foreign nationals had come home through Clark³⁵⁸. On 29 March 1973 the last US combat and support troops left South Vietnam, and MACV was disestablished. A Defense Attaché Office under Major General John E. Murray, with a few dozen military personnel and civilian contractors, took over the management of American military aid. The United States still maintained air power in Thailand and the Seventh Fleet offshore, and continued to bomb in Cambodia. The question of Americans missing in action, about whom Hanoi gave little information, would keep the war alive in American politics for two decades.

#### ◆ Case–Church and the end of American combat in Indochina
*Jun – 7 Nov 1973 · Washington; Cambodia · changed from history · EV000-86*
*Initiative: neither*

In June 1973 Congress passed the Case–Church amendment, which barred any US military activity in or over Vietnam, Laos and Cambodia without congressional approval from 15 August 1973³³⁵. Until that day the B-52s went on bombing Cambodia, which had continued since the Paris Agreement, in support of Lon Nol's forces around Phnom Penh. Historically on 6 August a B-52 dropped its bombs on the town of Neak Luong on the Mekong, killing about 137 people and wounding about 268, because its navigator had failed to set the offset for the radar beacon on which the strike was aimed. In this war no beacon was used: the J navigated and released on its own GPS, and the strike that night fell on its intended box in the forest east of the river. Neak Luong was not bombed, and the American ambassador in Phnom Penh did not have to pay its families a hundred dollars a death²⁶⁴.

On 15 August the bombing of Cambodia ended, and with it the last American combat missions in Indochina. The Js went home to the Strategic Air Command's alert pads; Sorrell's cell at Tan Son Nhut, which had been folded into the Defense Attaché Office's intelligence branch in March, was disbanded in September, and its folders for every firebase and district town in the South went to Clark Air Base and then to the Defense Mapping Agency. The Joint General Staff asked to keep a coordinate cell of its own; there was nothing for such a cell to send coordinates to, since no aircraft the VNAF flew could carry a guided bomb, and the request was not pursued²³²²⁶⁴. On 7 November Congress passed the War Powers Resolution over Nixon's veto, limiting the President's power to commit forces to hostilities without Congress. Nixon's promise to Thieu of a forceful response to a major violation was now beyond the President's power to keep without Congress, and Congress would not give it. The sanctuary constraint, which had shaped the war since 1964, now covered the whole of Indochina and was written into law.

#### Resolution 21 and the North's rebuilding
*Oct 1973 – 1974 · Hanoi; the Laotian panhandle and the western Highlands · historical · EV000-87*
In October 1973 the 21st Plenum of the Party Central Committee in Hanoi resolved that the revolution in the South must continue by the path of revolutionary violence, and that the PAVN should respond to Saigon's operations and prepare for a decisive offensive. The PAVN rebuilt through 1973 and 1974: it improved the strategic road along the western side of South Vietnam east of the Truong Son, extended the fuel pipeline into the South, built up stocks of ammunition and armour in the base areas and grouped its divisions into army corps, the 1st Corps in October 1973 and the 4th Corps on 20 July 1974 among them³⁵⁹. It was the North's own decision, on its own reading of the American political collapse and Saigon's weakness, and it was taken while Saigon still held the initiative in the countryside.

#### Saigon declares the war resumed; the Paracels
*Jan 1974 · Saigon; the Paracel Islands · historical · EV000-88*
In January 1974 Thieu announced that the war had resumed and that the Paris Agreement no longer bound his forces³³⁵. On 19 January a Chinese naval force defeated South Vietnamese ships in the Paracel Islands, and China took control of the islands³⁶⁰. The United States did not intervene. The clash showed how isolated Saigon had become: its ally would not act, and a great power on its northern horizon was willing to take territory from it.

#### The aid cuts and the shortages
*25 Jan – 9 Aug 1974 · South Vietnam and Washington · changed from history · EV000-89*
On 25 January 1974 supplies of ammunition and fuel to all ARVN units were cut because of budget limits. US military aid fell from about $1.1 billion in fiscal year 1974 to an appropriation of about $700 million for fiscal year 1975, the July 1974–June 1975 year, the figure given by source as $700–750 million³⁵⁷. Major General Murray of the Defense Attaché Office warned that cuts in support could roughly be equated with loss of territory, and that aid of $600 million would mean writing the South off³⁶⁰. The ARVN rationed artillery rounds, grounded aircraft and helicopters for lack of fuel and parts, and cut patrols; the soldiers' pay lost most of its value to inflation, and desertion rose. The economy suffered from the world oil shock of late 1973 and the loss of American spending.

In this war the ration fell on an army that had been built for ten years around the belt-fed gun and the 30-round magazine, four years longer than the historical one. The Joint General Staff's directive of February 1974 limited each infantry squad to its basic load for a month's operations, and the squad gun went first: about 200 rounds a gun for a month, which is what an M249 fires at the rapid rate in two minutes. Battalion commanders who had learned their trade breaking contacts with the squad gun's volume found that the 1974 ration let them do it about twice in a month, and the advisers' successors at the Defense Attaché Office reported that the infantry, rather than fight with aimed rifle fire as their fathers had fought with the Garand, avoided contacts it could not break with volume. The marksmen, whose match 7.62mm had a separate line, were rationed harder still; by the summer about half the M110A2s in I and II Corps were in arms rooms for want of ammunition. Divisions began to hoard linked 5.56mm in their own ammunition points against the ration, against JGS orders, and by the end of the year the 1st, 2nd and 3rd Divisions held about 9 million rounds outside the corps depots between them²⁸³. On 9 August 1974 Nixon resigned over Watergate, and Gerald Ford succeeded him³⁶⁰. The President who had given Thieu written promises was gone.

#### The ARVN's last offensives
*12 Feb – 2 May 1974 · Tri Phap, Dinh Tuong Province; Svay Rieng, Cambodia · historical · EV000-90*
In the spring of 1974 the ARVN still had the strength to attack. From 12 February to 4 May it struck the PAVN base at Tri Phap in Dinh Tuong Province in the Delta. From 27 March to 2 May, in the last major ARVN offensive of the war, III Corps attacked the PAVN base areas around Svay Rieng in Cambodia, claiming more than 1,200 PAVN killed against fewer than 100 ARVN³⁶⁰. They were the work of an army still able to win local battles when it had the ammunition and air support to do so. They did not change the balance, and after the spring the ARVN was on the defensive everywhere.

#### ◆ The war of attrition of 1974
*16 May – 10 Dec 1974 · The Iron Triangle; Thuong Duc, Duc Duc and Phu Loc in I Corps · changed from history · EV000-91*
*Initiative: enemy*

From May 1974 the PAVN took the initiative with a series of "strategic raids" designed to grind down the ARVN and test its reserves. In III Corps the PAVN 9th Division attacked Rach Bap in the Iron Triangle from 16 May; the ARVN retook it only in November. In I Corps a PAVN regiment overran An Hoa and attacked the district town of Thuong Duc from 18 July; the Airborne Division held the heights around it in a costly victory that lasted until November. At Duc Duc, from 18 July to 4 October, the ARVN 3rd Division suffered 4,700 casualties. From 28 August to 10 December the PAVN seized hills near Phu Loc that closed Phu Bai airfield to traffic and cut Highway 1, and the ARVN recovered them at heavy cost. In 1974 South Vietnam lost about 30,000 killed; the US estimated about 61,000 PAVN and VC killed³⁶⁰.

The weapons the ARVN carried in these fights were better than the PAVN's, as they had been since 1964, and it mattered less than at any time since. On the ridges west of Thuong Duc in September the 1st and 2nd Airborne Brigades took and retook Hill 1062 against the PAVN 304th Division's 66th Regiment; the Airborne's marksmen killed the 66th's spotters and gunners on the facing slopes at 500–700 metres, and its squads held the crest against three night assaults with the squad guns rationed to a belt each for the night. They held, and they bled, because the PAVN's 130mm guns and 122mm rockets were not rationed and the ARVN's 105s and 155s were²⁸³.

They bled in a second way that history's Airborne did not. Since An Loc, Kontum and the Lam Son bases the ARVN's I Corps had been taught, by success, to hold a besieged position and let the bombs break the siege. At Thuong Duc the district town's garrison, the 79th Ranger Battalion, dug in and waited, as its officers had seen the 23rd Division wait at Kontum, for a siege box that no longer existed; the 304th Division's regiment dug its trenches to the wire in eleven days without a bomb falling on them, and the town fell on 7 August. The Airborne's counter-attack on the heights was planned by officers who had held Lolo and Sophia in 1971 under the Js, and its first assaults on Hill 1062 went forward in the expectation that the PAVN's positions on the crest would be broken from the air first; the VNAF's A-37s and F-5As, flying in the monsoon cloud with unguided bombs, could not do it. I estimate that the Airborne Division's losses in the heights were perhaps a tenth higher than historically, a judgment I hold loosely, and I keep the 3rd Division's 4,700 at Duc Duc and South Vietnam's dead for the year at about 30,000³⁶⁰²⁶⁴. The ARVN, unable to replace its losses or its expended ammunition, had committed its strategic reserve, the Airborne and Marine Divisions, to static defence in I Corps, and had nothing left to move.

#### ◆ The fall of Phuoc Long
*12 Dec 1974 – 6 Jan 1975 · Phuoc Long Province (Phuoc Binh and Song Be), III Corps · changed from history · EV000-92*
*Initiative: enemy*

On 12 December 1974 the PAVN 4th Corps under Hoang Cam, with the 7th Division, the new 3rd Division and independent regiments including the 141st, 165th, 201st and 271st, and two armoured companies with 14 T-54s, attacked Phuoc Long Province on the Cambodian border. It was defended mainly by Regional Force battalions, reinforced by the 1st and 2nd Battalions, 7th Regiment and the 1st Battalion, 9th Regiment of the 5th Division and the 81st Airborne Ranger Battalion³⁵⁹. The province chief's plan for the defence of Phuoc Binh, drafted in 1973 by officers who had served at An Loc, was a plan to hold the town for six to eight weeks while the bombers broke the siege; it named the bomber cells by the call signs Hollingsworth's men had used in 1972. On 13 December the province chief asked III Corps for "the B-52 support provided for in the plan", and III Corps forwarded the request to the Joint General Staff, which forwarded it to the Defense Attaché Office, which had no means to answer it²⁶⁴.

On 18 December, in the meeting at the Independence Palace where Thieu and the Joint General Staff decided not to send the general reserve to Phuoc Long, the request was on the table, and the decision taken was historical: the Airborne Division was committed in I Corps, and Thieu would not risk a second division in a province he judged he could not hold without American bombers. In this war the judgment was put in those words, and with more conviction, because the Republic's officers had seen at An Loc and Kontum what American bombers could do for a besieged town and knew exactly what they were not getting²⁶⁴. The last ARVN post fell on 6 January 1975, the first provincial capital lost permanently since the Paris Agreement. The ARVN lost more than 1,000 killed and 2,444 captured; the PAVN about 1,300 killed and wounded. Saigon did not try to retake it. Washington made no military response³⁵⁹. In Hanoi, where a strategy conference sat from 18 December to 8 January, Phuoc Long answered the question the leadership had set itself: the United States would not return³⁶⁰. The plan for a two-year campaign to end the war in 1976 was adapted to strike in the Central Highlands in 1975. In Saigon the answer was received at the same moment and in the same words, and it fell on an army whose defence of every besieged town had been built for a decade around the aircraft that would not come.

#### The fall of Ban Me Thuot
*10–12 Mar 1975 · Ban Me Thuot, Darlac Province · historical · EV000-93*
For Campaign 275 the PAVN Central Highlands Front under Lieutenant General Hoang Minh Thao, directed by the chief of the general staff, General Van Tien Dung, massed the 10th, 316th and 320A Divisions with the 3rd and 968th Divisions, tanks, artillery and anti-aircraft units. Diversionary attacks on Pleiku and Kontum, false radio traffic and local rumours drew the attention of the II Corps commander, Major General Pham Van Phu, who discounted repeated warnings and kept his forces in the north. At 02:00 on 10 March the PAVN attacked Ban Me Thuot, defended mainly by the 53rd Regiment of the 23rd Division with Rangers and Regional Forces; the town was in PAVN hands by 11–12 March. The counter-attack by the rest of the 23rd Division, lifted in by helicopter, was broken. The PAVN admitted 600–800 killed and 2,416 wounded; the Vietnamese official history counts 28,514 ARVN put out of action, 4,502 killed and 16,822 captured. II Corps lost more than three-quarters of its combat units³⁶¹. Ban Me Thuot was the strategic surprise of the war's last campaign.

#### The collapse of I Corps
*13–30 Mar 1975 · Quang Tri, Hue and Da Nang · historical · EV000-94*
On 13 March Thieu ordered Truong to give up most of I Corps and withdrew the Airborne Division towards Saigon as a reserve. On 20 March he ordered Truong to hold only Da Nang; on 29 March he ordered him to turn back and retake it, which Truong refused. The contradictory orders, given as the PAVN 2nd Corps with the 304th, 324th and 325th Divisions attacked from the north and west and the 2nd Division and 52nd Brigade struck in Quang Ngai and Quang Tin, broke I Corps. Northern Quang Tri was lost by 18 March; Hue was surrounded by the evening of 25 March and abandoned; about 6,000 soldiers of the 2nd Division deserted on 28 March; and the PAVN entered Da Nang on the afternoon of 29 March. Soldiers abandoned their units to find their families, and the sea evacuation from Da Nang became chaos, with people fighting to board the ships. I Corps had about 134,000 men against more than 75,000 PAVN and VC; three ARVN infantry divisions and the 147th Marine Brigade ceased to exist, and about 58,722 soldiers were captured at Hue. The PAVN took 129 aircraft, 179 armoured vehicles and 327 artillery pieces³⁶². The best corps in the ARVN, under its best commander, had been destroyed without a decisive battle.

#### The retreat from the Highlands
*14–25 Mar 1975 · Pleiku and Kontum to the coast along Route 7, through Cheo Reo · historical · EV000-95*
On 14 March at Cam Ranh, Thieu ordered Phu to abandon Pleiku and Kontum and pull II Corps back to the coast to defend the populated regions. The chief of the Joint General Staff, General Cao Van Vien, warned against Route 19 because of the French disaster at the Mang Yang Pass in 1954; Thieu chose Route 7, a disused road in poor condition. The retreat was ordered verbally and kept secret, even from many of the units and the province chiefs; within days some 400,000 civilians had joined the columns. The PAVN ambushed the column near Cheo Reo, where fighting ended on 19 March, and shelled it along the road. Only the 6th Ranger Battalion and the 19th Armoured Cavalry Squadron arrived in good order; an estimated 155,000 civilians were killed or abducted on the "convoy of tears", and RVNAF air strikes hit civilians too³⁶¹. It was the decision that lost the war in the shortest time: II Corps, which had held the Highlands through every offensive since 1965, was destroyed on the road in ten days.

#### Xuan Loc
*9–21 Apr 1975 · Xuan Loc, Long Khanh Province, northeast of Saigon · changed from history · EV000-96*
On 9 April the PAVN 4th Corps under Major General Hoang Cam, with the 6th, 7th and 341st Divisions and tanks and artillery, attacked Xuan Loc, the road junction northeast of Saigon on Route 1. The town was held by the ARVN 18th Division under Brigadier General Le Minh Dao, with the 1st Airborne Brigade, armoured units, Rangers and Regional Forces, and air support from Bien Hoa and Tan Son Nhut. The 18th Division held for twelve days against repeated assaults; on 12 April two BLU-82 bombs were dropped on PAVN troops, reportedly killing about 200³⁶³.

In this war the 18th Division fought at Xuan Loc with what was left of a decade's equipment and, by April, with the ammunition the Joint General Staff could send it from the Long Binh depot, which by then was most of what remained in III Corps. Its squads had their squad guns and the belts to feed them for the first time since January 1974, because Dao drew everything Long Binh would give him; its marksmen held the edges of the town against the 341st Division's infantry crossing the rubber and the open ground east of the town on 9–11 April, and the 43rd Regiment's account of the first three days records the PAVN assault battalions going to ground at 400 metres under fire they could not locate. It held for twelve days, as it held historically³⁶³. The ammunition that might have let it hold longer had gone in March, in the divisional hoards at Hue, Da Nang and Qui Nhon that were abandoned when I Corps and the coast collapsed: about 9 million rounds of linked 5.56mm and most of the match 7.62mm in the country, captured intact²⁸³.

The PAVN changed its approach, bypassed and isolated the town, and on 19 April the Joint General Staff ordered the withdrawal; Xuan Loc was abandoned on 21 April, and the 3rd Battalion of the 1st Airborne Brigade was destroyed in the withdrawal at Suoi Ca. The PAVN claimed 2,036 ARVN killed or wounded and 2,731 captured and admitted 460 killed and 1,428 wounded; Americans estimated about 5,000 PAVN casualties and 37 tanks destroyed³⁶³. I do not adjust these figures; the squad weapons raised the price of each assault and the PAVN answered, as it did historically, by not making more of them²⁸³. On 17 April Phnom Penh fell to the Khmer Rouge. Xuan Loc was the ARVN's last stand, and showed what a well-led division could still do; it bought twelve days.

#### Thieu resigns; the last governments of the Republic
*10–28 Apr 1975 · Saigon and Washington · historical · EV000-97*
On 10 April President Ford asked Congress for $722 million in emergency military aid to rebuild some of the destroyed South Vietnamese forces; Congress would not vote it. On 23 April Ford told an audience at Tulane University that the war was "finished as far as America is concerned". On 21 April Thieu resigned in a televised address denouncing the United States for abandoning his country, and handed power to Vice President Tran Van Huong. Thieu left the country a few days later. On 28 April Huong handed the presidency to General Duong Van Minh, in the hope that Hanoi would negotiate with him; it would not. On 28 April captured A-37s flown by defecting pilots bombed Tan Son Nhut³⁶⁴.

#### The fall of Saigon
*29–30 Apr 1975 · Saigon · historical · EV000-98*
By late April five PAVN corps, Group 232 and the 1st, 2nd, 3rd and 4th Corps, with 15 infantry divisions and three tank regiments, encircled Saigon in the Ho Chi Minh Campaign. Rockets and artillery hit Tan Son Nhut on the morning of 29 April; two US Marine guards, Corporal Charles McMahon and Lance Corporal Darwin Judge, were killed, the last American servicemen killed in action in Vietnam. At 10:51 Operation Frequent Wind began: Marine and Air Force helicopters lifted 395 Americans and more than 4,000 Vietnamese from the Defense Attaché Office compound, and 978 Americans and about 1,100 Vietnamese from the embassy roof and grounds, to the ships of the Seventh Fleet. Ambassador Graham Martin left on a helicopter at 04:58 on 30 April³⁶⁴. Thousands of Vietnamese who had worked with the Americans were left behind.

At 10:24 on 30 April Minh announced an unconditional surrender. PAVN tanks crashed through the gates of the Presidential Palace, Tank 390 through the main gate, and at 11:30 a soldier raised the flag of the Front over the palace³⁶⁴. The Republic of Vietnam had ceased to exist. Saigon was renamed Ho Chi Minh City; Vietnam was formally reunified as the Socialist Republic of Vietnam in July 1976.

#### How the phase ends
The war ends on 30 April 1975 with the fall of Saigon and the unconditional surrender of the Republic of Vietnam, on the historical timeline: rung (a), collapse. The Paris Agreement had removed the American forces and kept the PAVN in the South; Congress had written the end of American combat and the limits on the President into law; American military aid had fallen; and the government in Saigon, more stable than at any time since 1963 but narrowly based, economically weakened and dependent on American supply, faced an enemy that had rebuilt after 1972 and chose its moment. The decisive blows were Hanoi's, at Phuoc Long and Ban Me Thuot; the decisive errors were Saigon's, in the order to abandon the Highlands and the contradictory orders to I Corps.

Hanoi's posture never changed. It was committed to armed reunification throughout, from the Ninth Plenum of December 1963 to the Ho Chi Minh Campaign, through the losses of 1965–68 and 1972, and it accepted the Paris Agreement as a step towards that end. The US war in Vietnam, from the advisory years to 1973, cost more than 58,000 American dead. The Vietnamese dead, military and civilian, northern and southern, are counted in the millions by most estimates, and the record of them is less precise than the record of the Americans. That is the war as it happened, and the war the exercise begins from.

The squad guns and the marksmen's rifles of the ARVN, and about 9 million rounds of linked 5.56mm from the divisional hoards of I and II Corps, are in the PAVN's hands by the end of April 1975, with the carbines of a million men²⁸³.

The Republic's defence of every besieged town had been built since 1971 around American bombers and American coordinates, and both were gone by the end of 1973²⁶⁴.

### Mechanism

The counter-directional finding of this memorandum is borne out in this phase, and it is the reason the provision is not a net gain to the Republic's chances. From Lam Son 719 to Kontum the ARVN learned that it held besieged towns with American bombers measured onto the enemy by American analysts. The lesson was true, and both halves of it left in 1973: the Js on 15 August, the coordinate cell in September. Nothing the VNAF flew could carry a guided bomb, and nothing the Joint General Staff owned could make a coordinate a guided bomb could use. The army that met the strategic raids of 1974 had been trained by success to fight a kind of battle it could no longer win. At Thuong Duc the Rangers dug in and waited, and the town fell; the Airborne assaulted Hill 1062 expecting the crest to be broken from the air first, and bled perhaps a tenth more than history's Airborne. At Phuoc Long the defence plan named bomber cells that no longer existed.

The provision did not change the date or the manner of the collapse. Hanoi's decisions at Phuoc Long and Ban Me Thuot, and Saigon's at Pleiku and in I Corps, were taken on the historical grounds; the ARVN's dependence on American fire and supply was already decisive without the bombers, and the bombers deepened it at the margin. I keep the end state at the historical one, and the end vector at historical on every headline variable: the deeper dependence of 1973 is real, and it is erased by the collapse it helped to bring, which is the same collapse.

There is one gain in this phase, and it is a small and real one. The last American bombing of the war, over Cambodia in the summer of 1973, did not bomb Neak Luong, because the J navigated on GPS and no navigator forgot a beacon offset. About 137 people who died historically did not die there.

### Enemy response and initiative

The PAVN read the bombers' departure as it read everything about them: correctly and quickly. Resolution 21 of October 1973 and the strategic raids of 1974 were planned historically on the premise that the Americans would not return; in this war the General Staff added a specific premise, that besieging a town was again possible, and the 304th Division's regiment at Thuong Duc dug its trenches to the wire in eleven days without a bomb falling on them. Hoang Cam's 4th Corps at Phuoc Long did the same. The test of Phuoc Long was the test Hanoi had set itself historically, whether the United States would return, and the answer was the same.

### Causal chains

**C005-7** [war-shaping] The siege box is decisive at An Loc and Kontum in 1972 → the ARVN writes its defence of every besieged town around American bombers, and Phuoc Long's plan of 1973 names the bomber cells by their 1972 call signs → when Phuoc Long is attacked in December 1974 its defenders and Thieu know precisely what they lack, and Thieu declines to send a division to a province he judges cannot be held without American bombers → Saigon reads Phuoc Long as Hanoi does, as the proof that the Americans will not return, a month before Ban Me Thuot, and the order of March 1975 to abandon the Highlands is taken by a president who had already concluded his army could not hold a besieged town alone.

### Downstream

There is no later phase. The war ends on 30 April 1975, at rung (a).

### Clock

The American clock had run out before this phase began, and the provision did not wind it. Case–Church ended the bombing on 15 August 1973 on the historical date. The one effect I can name is the absence of Neak Luong from the summer's news, a small and real reduction in what the last weeks of American bombing cost the administration's credibility, and it changed no vote.

## §4.H Historical comparison

The cumulative force now holds the land reform and the Commission of 001, the police and the chiefs of 002, the reconnaissance of 003, the small arms of 004 and the bombers of 005, and against the historical war it has changed the following, with this memorandum's share stated plainly.

**Sieges.** The strongest change this memorandum makes is in the war's sieges, and in them it is large. Con Thien lifted three weeks early; Khe Sanh's trenches never reached the wire and the PAVN withdrew two weeks early at perhaps half again its acknowledged losses; An Loc's penetration on 12 May was destroyed by noon; Kontum was secure five days early. I estimate that across these four battles and Lam Son 719 the bombers saved about 400–700 American and about 1,000–2,000 South Vietnamese lives, and that the PAVN lost perhaps 8,000–15,000 more men than historically²⁶⁴. The figures are judgments built on the historical losses and the change in what the bombs could reach, and I hold them loosely; the direction and the order of magnitude I hold firmly.

**The air war in the North.** Linebacker II lost about six bombers rather than fifteen, and about twenty-five crewmen rather than sixty-six; Bach Mai and Kham Thien Street were not struck, and I estimate perhaps 400–700 fewer civilian dead in Hanoi and Haiphong²⁶⁴. The Thanh Hoa bridge fell in April 1972 rather than May. Nothing in the North fell before 1972 that did not fall historically, because Washington did not allow it.

**The Trail.** The bombers destroyed about 210 crossings in 1966 and several thousand more by 1972, and they raised the cost of every crossing of the panhandle. They did not lower what arrived in the South below what the South's forces needed, and by teaching Group 559 to build a net with no single point of failure, they made Commando Hunt's interdiction points less productive than history's. On the Trail the honest comparison is a draw that cost the enemy labour and us bombs.

**Lives elsewhere.** The first Arc Light collision's eight dead did not die; Neak Luong's 137 did not die. The survey control given to the artillery in 1965 improved every fire mission for eight years, and I cannot count what it saved.

**What did not change.** Every main-force battle the enemy chose in the open or under canopy went as it went historically. Tet's political verdict, the decision of 31 March, the withdrawal, the Paris terms, Case–Church, the fall of Phuoc Long, Ban Me Thuot and Saigon on 30 April 1975 all stand. Across the five memoranda the war in the hamlets has changed more than anywhere else, and this memorandum did not touch it.

## §4.N Net assessment and end state

The outcome in its own terms: a war in which the United States, from 1965, possessed a precision heavy bomber able to destroy any fixed target it could measure, in any weather, and in which it used that bomber decisively in the five sieges of the war and nowhere else that mattered. The Republic of Vietnam fell on 30 April 1975, on the historical timeline. The end state remains rung (a), collapse.

Rung (b), dependent survival, is not reachable with this force, and the reason is the reason in every earlier memorandum, sharpened. The Republic falls when the American combat forces leave, on the historical timetable, and nothing in the cumulative force keeps them there longer or makes their departure survivable. This provision added the most to the Republic's strength in exactly the kind of battle that the Paris Agreement took away from it: the defence of a besieged town under American air power. It made the ARVN better at a kind of war it would not be allowed to fight, and less ready for the kind it would. The distance between (c) and (d) is the whole war, and this provision moved the Republic away from (d), not toward it, while making its performance at (c) in 1972 more convincing than history's.

What would move the end state is now clearer than it was after Memorandum 004. The political deficit is largely met where the government holds the night (001, 002). The finding and fixing deficits are met against fixed targets and the siege, and not against the main force under canopy (003, 005). The deficit that decides the war is the Republic's ability to defend itself against a conventional PAVN offensive without American participation, and the provisions so far have given it either nothing for that war or instruments it cannot keep. A provision that gives the RVNAF its own means of finding and striking a besieged enemy, aircraft and weapons it owns and can sustain, or that changes the American political settlement of 1973, would address it. A provision that strengthens the American force further will not.

I do not propose a redraw of the phases. The war's shape has not outgrown them: every phase boundary still marks a turn that happened on its date.

## §5 Delta ledger

**Forces gained.** P005a: the B-52J replacing every B-52, about 600 airframes, Southeast Asia deployment on the historical scale. P005b: GBU-31 at about 8,000–12,000 kits a year. P005c: GBU-38 at about 15,000–25,000 kits a year. P005d: Quickstrike at about 3,000–6,000 mines a year. A coordinate cell of about 60 men at MACV J-2 and Seventh Air Force²³².

**Forces lost.** About six B-52Js in Linebacker II²⁶⁴; no B-52 lost in the first Arc Light mission. The coordinate cell disbanded in September 1973; the Js withdrawn on 15 August 1973.

**Variables moved at phase ends.**
- ENEMY.engagement_control: −1 at the end of P3, P4 and P5 (the PAVN loses the set-piece siege of a measured position as a way of choosing its battles; restored at P6 when the bombers are gone).
- ARVN.self_sufficiency: −1 at the end of P5 (the defence of besieged towns built around American bombers and coordinates); returned to historical at P6, erased by the collapse.
- No band moved in P1 or P2; no band moved in P6.

**New categories.** Measured-coordinate targeting as a theater function, from 1964. Survey control on GPS truth for the artillery, from 1965.

## §6 Changed / not changed

### What has changed

- **The siege became the enemy's worst battle.** At Con Thien, Khe Sanh, An Loc and Kontum, the Global Hawk and the coordinate cell turned every gun pit and trench into a coordinate overnight and the J put a GBU-38 on each, 400 metres from the defenders. Sieges lifted weeks early, perhaps 1,400–2,700 American and South Vietnamese lives were saved, and the PAVN lost perhaps 8,000–15,000 more men than historically²⁶⁴. That is a transformation of one kind of battle, and the largest effect this memorandum records.
- **The enemy stopped laying sieges between 1968 and 1972.** After Khe Sanh the PAVN would not besiege a position the Americans could measure. That effect appears in no statistic and was the bombers' largest contribution from 1969 to 1971.
- **The last air campaigns cost less.** Linebacker II lost about six Js rather than fifteen B-52s, and about twenty-five crewmen rather than sixty-six; Bach Mai, Kham Thien Street and Neak Luong were not struck. The Thanh Hoa bridge fell on Freedom Train's first night.
- **The artillery was laid on true coordinates from 1965**, because the coordinate cell discovered the map error that would otherwise have turned guided bombs into precise fratricide.
- **The Republic's army became more dependent.** I Corps from 1971 and the whole ARVN from 1972 built the defence of besieged towns around American bombers and American coordinates, and lost both in 1973. At Thuong Duc and Phuoc Long that cost them.

### What has not changed

- **Nothing under the canopy or in the hamlets.** The J found nothing; COSVN was never located; the main-force regiments chose their battles in the forest as before, and the war in the hamlets was untouched.
- **The Trail delivered what the South needed.** Every measured crossing died in a night and was replaced by several unmeasured ones; the net became more redundant than history's, and tonnage arriving stayed above the requirement.
- **Washington's map.** The Js did not strike north of the 20th parallel until 1972, nor Haiphong until May 1972, nor Hanoi until December 1972. The modelled constraints held throughout, and the provision's greatest uses waited on them.
- **Tet's verdict, the withdrawal and the Paris terms.** The victory at Khe Sanh was won where the war was not decided; the decision of 31 March, the withdrawal timetable and the January 1973 terms stand.
- **The end.** Saigon falls on 30 April 1975, at rung (a).

### Trajectory Log entry

Memorandum 005 replaced every B-52 with the B-52J and gave it GPS bombs and modern mines, and found that precision changes what dies, not who wins. A coordinate cell built from the U-2S's imagery made every fixed target a one-night kill, and Washington refused to use it in the North for seven years. In the panhandle the bombers cut every measured crossing in 1966 and taught Group 559 to build a net with no single point of failure, so the Trail delivered as before. In the sieges the effect was transforming: Con Thien, Khe Sanh, An Loc and Kontum became the enemy's worst battles, sparing perhaps 1,400–2,700 allied lives, and after Khe Sanh the PAVN stopped laying sieges until 1972 forced it to. Linebacker II lost six bombers, not fifteen, and Bach Mai was not hit. But the ARVN learned to hold besieged towns with American bombers it could never own, and in 1974 it waited at Thuong Duc and Phuoc Long for bombers that did not come. Saigon falls on 30 April 1975, at rung (a).

### Positions recorded

- **POS005-3** · vars: ENEMY.engagement_control, FIX.capability · In this war from Khe Sanh onward the PAVN does not lay a set-piece siege to a position the Americans can measure, until the conventional offensive of 1972 forces it to besiege An Loc and Kontum; it loses that one way of choosing its battles while the B-52J and the coordinate cell are present, and regains it when they leave in 1973 (005 §4.P3, §4.P5; EV000-41, EV000-76, EV000-77)
- **POS005-4** · vars: TRAIL.capacity, TRAIL.tonnage_arriving · In this war the panhandle's fixed crossings were cut by the B-52J from April 1966, and Group 559 rebuilt the net as braided fords, causeways and pontoons with no single point of failure; from 1966 the Trail is more redundant than history's, and precision strikes on crossings are never counted as reductions in tonnage arriving (005 §4.P2; EV005-2, EV000-55)

### Persons recorded

- **Edwin T. Sorrell** · Major, later Lieutenant Colonel, USAF; chief of the coordinate cell at Tan Son Nhut from March 1964 to its disbandment in September 1973, formerly a geodesist of the Aeronautical Chart and Information Center · invented in 005 · Found the map datum error and ruled that no map coordinate would be given to a guided bomb; built the folders of 1964–65 and ran the siege box at Con Thien, Khe Sanh, Lam Son 719, An Loc and Kontum

Source: "B-52 played major role in Operation Linebacker II" — Andersen Air Force Base, https://www.andersen.af.mil/News/Article/1398659/b-52-played-major-role-in-operation-linebacker-ii/ — about 40 B-52Ds at U-Tapao at the end of 1971, an eventual 54; nearly 50 Ds with the 43rd Strategic Wing on Guam; B-52G deployments of 1972; Arc Light at about 1,500 sorties a month after 14 February 1972; Olive 2 lost 22 November 1972
13. Source: "Arc Light" — Air & Space Forces Magazine, January 2009, https://www.airandspaceforces.com/article/0109arc/ — first mission 18 June 1965 from Andersen with two aircraft lost in a collision; B-52D "Big Belly" 84 bombs internal and 24 external; bases Andersen, U-Tapao and Kadena; more than 126,000 sorties 1965–73; about 800 sorties a month by mid-1967 and 1,800 a month from February 1968; 210 B-52s in East Asia in 1972 under Bullet Shot, 155 at Andersen; three-ship cells; target boxes 1.2 by 0.6 miles; ground-directed radar bombing from seven sites by June 1967; Soviet trawler watching take-offs off Guam; six of 99 lost on the third night of Linebacker II, fifteen in eleven days

Source: "New Power for the B-52" — Air & Space Forces Magazine, https://www.airandspaceforces.com/article/new-power-for-the-b-52/ — F130 about 30 percent more fuel-efficient than the TF33; no change to thrust or speed; eight engines retained in four nacelles to avoid wing and cockpit redesign; about 650 F130s for 76 aircraft; far fewer maintenance hours, no off-wing overhaul before retirement; new nacelle, wiring, hydraulics, power and cooling, digital throttles

Source: "Joint Direct Attack Munition GBU-31/32/38" fact sheet — U.S. Air Force, af.mil, https://www.af.mil/About-Us/Fact-Sheets/Display/Article/104572/joint-direct-attack-munition-gbu-313238/ — GBU-31(V)1/B 2,036 lb and 152.7 in; GBU-31(V)3/B 2,115 lb and 148.6 in; GBU-38 558 lb and 92.6 in; wingspans 25 in and 14 in; Mk 84/BLU-109, Mk 83/BLU-110, Mk 82/BLU-111 bodies; range up to 15 miles; release ceiling 45,000+ ft; CEP 5 m or less with GPS, 30 m or less for up to 100 seconds of free flight GPS-denied; 9.6 m in initial operational test; transfer alignment from the aircraft; about $22,000 a tail kit (FY2007); development from 1992, first delivery 1997, combat debut in Allied Force; carried by B-52H among others

Source: "B-52 Tested 2,000lb Quickstrike-ER Winged Standoff Naval Mines During Valiant Shield" — The War Zone, https://www.twz.com/23705/b-52-tested-2000lb-quickstrike-er-winged-standoff-naval-mines-during-valiant-shield — Quickstrike-ER releasable at 40–50 miles; JDAM-ER wing kit; standard Quickstrike laid below about 500 ft at about 300 knots or less with ballute or retarding fins, for depths of about 300 ft or less; detection device in the tail; settable activity windows and signature discrimination; B-52H shown with four QS-ER
186. Source: "Air Weapons: Quickstrike" — StrategyPage, https://www.strategypage.com/htmw/htairw/articles/20070123.aspx — Mk 62 is basically a 500 lb bomb with a sensor package at the rear; three sensor packages detecting pressure, magnetism and vibration; programmable to fire only on ships meeting set criteria; B-52 release from about 1,000 ft at 500–600 km/h
187. Source: "U.S. Navy Mines" fact file — U.S. Navy, https://www.navy.mil/Resources/Fact-Files/Display-FactFiles/Article/2167942/us-navy-mines/ — Mk 62 converted 500 lb bomb, Mk 63 converted 1,000 lb bomb, Mk 65 2,000 lb thin-walled mine case, aircraft-laid in shallow water, deployed 1983; Mk 64 Mod 5 Quickstrike-ER on a 2,000 lb bomb body with wing and tail kits, delivered at mid-range by Navy and Air Force aircraft
188. Source: "Mk-65 Quick Strike Mine" — GlobalSecurity.org, https://www.globalsecurity.org/military/systems/munitions/mk65.htm — 2,390 lb; 128 in long; 29 in across fins; depth up to 600 ft; magnetic, seismic and pressure TDDs on various models; settable arming delay, sterilisation and self-destruct; bottom mine against submarines and surface targets
189. Source: "Quickstrike" — GlobalSecurity.org, https://www.globalsecurity.org/military/systems/munitions/quickstrike.htm — Mk 57 TDD in Mk 63/64/65; Mk 71 TDD at Milestone III in 1995 with algorithms for detecting, classifying and localising ships including quiet diesel-electric submarines, fast patrol boats and air-cushion vehicles; Mk 75 safe-and-arming device and Mk 176 lithium battery in Mod 3 kits; mines respond to near-field ship signatures; inert Quickstrike-J and -ER laid in Valiant Shield 2016
190.
